<page-num>94</page-num>

# Catechism of the Scottish Master.

<note>A "Catechism" in Freemasonry is a memorized dialogue used to instruct members and verify that a brother has truly attained a specific rank.</note>

Q. Most Respectable Brother, what do you know in your capacity as a Scottish Master?
A. Most Puissant <note>original: *Très-puissant*. A formal title used to address the presiding officer of the lodge in high-degree Masonry.</note>, I know the great light of the third apartment.
Q. Where were you received?
A. In the high place.
Q. What path did you take?
A. That of the middle chamber, to the third apartment.
Q. Does this third apartment not have another name?
A. It is called Gibeon <note>original: *Gabaon*. A biblical city near Jerusalem. According to the Bible (1 Kings 3), King Solomon offered sacrifices there and received a vision from God before the Temple was completed.</note>; it is the high place where David and Solomon offered burnt offerings <note>original: *holocaustes*. Ancient religious sacrifices where an offering was completely consumed by fire.</note> to the Lord before the construction of the Temple.
Q. In what do you occupy yourself as a Scottish Master?
A. In erecting Altars and Tabernacles, and in furnishing them with precious ornaments.
Q. What are those ornaments?
A. The Ark of the Covenant, supported by two Cherubim who cover it with their wings, the brass table, the table of burnt offerings, the table of the showbread <note>original: *pains*. Referring to the "Bread of the Presence" placed on a golden table within the Tabernacle as described in Exodus.</note>, and the seven-branched candlestick.
Q. What does the Ark of the Covenant contain?

<summary>This page presents the opening of the Scottish Master's catechism, establishing the degree's symbolic connection to the biblical Gibeon and the sacred furnishings of the Tabernacle. It outlines the candidate's transition from the "middle chamber" of basic Masonry to the "third apartment" of higher knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Catechism, Scottish Master, Gibeon, Solomon's Temple, Ark of the Covenant, Tabernacle furnishings, Masonic ritual, burnt offerings</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal catechism of the Scottish Master degree. Having established the "high place" (Gibeon) and the general ornaments of the Tabernacle on the previous page, the text now moves into specific questions regarding the Ark of the Covenant and the divine presence.</meta>

->( 95 )<-

**A.** The Shekhinah <note>original: *Stékenna*. This is a phonetic transcription of the Hebrew term *Shekhinah*, which refers to the "dwelling" or divine presence of God. In Masonic tradition, it represents the light of divine wisdom.</note>, which established itself between the Cherubim that cover it with their wings, in the Holy of Holies, on the day of the Dedication, where it delivered its oracles.

**Q.** What was the primary law given by the Shekhinah?

**A.** The one that was given on Mount Sinai, kept ever since in the Ark, which is the first written law.

**Q.** Give me the sign of this law?

**A.** It is given by placing both hands on the head, with the fingers spread; which signifies the symbol of the Ten Commandments.

**Q.** In what form is the Shekhinah represented?

**A.** In the form of a peaceful Lamb, lying down, resting upon the Book of the Seven Seals <note>A reference to the Book of Revelation 5:1. In this Masonic context, the Lamb represents innocence and the redeemer, while the seven seals represent hidden mysteries or historical eras.</note>.

**Q.** Of what wood was the Ark constructed?

**A.** Of Shittim wood <note>original: *sétim*. Derived from the Hebrew *shittah*, this refers to acacia wood. According to the Bible, it was used for the Tabernacle and the Ark because it was considered "incorruptible" (rot-resistant).</note>, an incorruptible wood, overlaid with plates of gold.

**Q.** What were its proportions?

**A.** It was two and a half cubits <note>An ancient unit of measure based on the length of the forearm, approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> in length, a half in width, and the same in height.

**Q.** What was its shape?

**A.** That of a square chest with four rings of gold, and four staves of wood of

<summary>This page detailing the "Scottish Master" catechism describes the Ark of the Covenant, the Ten Commandments, and the symbolic representation of the Shekhinah as a lamb on the Book of Seven Seals. It emphasizes the sacred dimensions and materials, such as Shittim wood, used in the construction of the Ark.</summary>

<keywords>Shekhinah, Ark of the Covenant, Ten Commandments, Holy of Holies, Shittim wood, Acacia, Book of the Seven Seals, Masonic catechism, ritual symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the catechism of the Scottish Master, completing the description of the Ark of the Covenant's construction and detailing its contents, its historical travels through the Holy Land, and the arrangement of other sacred objects within the Tabernacle.</meta>

shittim wood <note>original: *sétim*. As noted previously, this refers to acacia wood.</note> to carry it, and it was covered with plates of gold.

**Q.** What did it contain?
**A.** The Shekhinah <note>original: *Stékenna*. The divine presence or "dwelling" of God.</note>, the tables of the law, Aaron’s rod, and the urn full of manna.

**Q.** In what place was it made, and by whom?
**A.** It was made at Horeb <note>Another name for Mount Sinai.</note> by Bezalel <note>original: *Beseleel*. The primary artisan of the Tabernacle, endowed by God with wisdom and skill (Exodus 31).</note> and Oholiab <note>original: *Ooltab*. A master craftsman and assistant to Bezalel.</note>. It departed from Horeb and passed to Moab, from Moab to Shechem, then to Shiloh; from Shiloh to the Temple of Dagon <note>A reference to the Ark's capture by the Philistines.</note>; from there into the house of Abinadab, from there to that of Obed-Edom, then to Kiriath-Jearim <note>original: *Cariathiarim*. A city where the Ark remained for twenty years.</note>, from Kiriath-Jearim to Jerusalem, then to holy Zion in the Temple.

**Q.** Where were the other ornaments placed, and what was their purpose?
**A.** The Bronze Table—or the grated Altar—was in front, near the Mercy Seat <note>original: *Propitiatoire*. The gold lid of the Ark, considered the place where God’s presence rested and spoke to the people.</note> from where God spoke. The Altar of Incense and the Altar of Burnt Offerings, one on each side, were used for offerings and sacrifices. The table for the Showbread <note>original: *pains de Proposition*. Twelve loaves of bread set out every Sabbath as an offering of gratitude from the twelve tribes.</note> was in front; the seven-branched Candelabrum <note>The Menorah.</note>, which was of pure gold, was in the middle; the other ornaments were for the most part of shittim wood, covered with plates of gold and bronze, worked with infinite art.

**Q.** What does the seven-branched Candelabrum represent?

<keywords>Ark of the Covenant, Shekhinah, Bezalel, Oholiab, Mercy Seat, Showbread, Menorah, biblical history</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the catechism of the Scottish Master degree. While the previous page focused on the physical history and contents of the Ark of the Covenant, this page transitions into the symbolic interpretation of the creation, the arduous journey of the initiate, and the moral trials required to achieve "the brilliant light."</meta>

->97<-
->Tome II. I<-

**A.** The seven days that God employed in the creation of the world, including the day of rest.
**Q.** How did you come to know all these things?
**A.** By the designs that I presented to Solomon, after a very arduous journey.
**Q.** What was the duration of this journey?
**A.** Three times twenty-seven times around the works <note>In Masonic symbolism, circumambulation (walking around the lodge) represents the progress of the initiate and the passage of time or celestial bodies.</note>.
**Q.** What does this arduous journey signify?
**A.** The construction of the work.
**Q.** Was the brilliant light the reward for the work?
**A.** No, Most Puissant <note>original: *Très-Puissant*. A formal title used for the presiding officer in certain high degrees of Freemasonry.</note>. Three much stronger trials acquired it for me.
**Q.** What are these trials?
**A.** The first was three Fellowcraft <note>original: *Compagnon*. The second degree of Freemasonry, focused on labor and education.</note> steps backward, to show us that we must not lack firmness on the path of virtue, despite the humiliations that may be required of us in this life. The second is an exact account of the progress I had made in Masonry, and of the practice of all the virtues that had been taught to me. The third was a voluntary engagement <note>Referring to a formal oath or obligation taken during the initiation ceremony.</note> that my heart accepted; my soul acknowledged, and my mouth pronounced.
**Q.** What has this engagement produced for you?

<summary>This page details a portion of the Scottish Master's catechism, focusing on the symbolic "arduous journey" of the initiate and the three moral trials required to attain spiritual light, emphasizing virtue and backward steps as a lesson in humility.</summary>
<keywords>Scottish Master, Solomon, Fellowcraft, Masonic trials, Masonic virtue, symbolic journey, Seven Days of Creation</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the catechism of the Scottish Master degree. Following the description of the candidate's trials and their entry into a state of mourning, the text now explains the ritual transition from sorrow to enlightenment and the candidate's ultimate elevation to the rank of Superintendent, replacing the fallen master, Adonhiram.</meta>

**A.** A tribute to the shades <note>original: *mânes*. Referring to the spirits or souls of the deceased, a term borrowed from Roman mythology.</note> of Adonhiram to mourn his death.
**Q.** What did this sorrow produce for you?
**A.** A word marked by a second circumference—an image of infinite Power—which predicts sadness and sorrow in Masonry, the collapse of our material edifice, and the spirit of a more perfect work through obedience.
**Q.** What is this word?
**A.** *Civi*, which means: "Bend the knee."
**Q.** Was this sorrow long?
**A.** After seven minutes, *Caki* was pronounced, which means: "Rise."
**Q.** What resulted from this word?
**A.** Surprise, radiance, the brilliant light, and the proclamation.
**Q.** What was this surprise?
**A.** My sight passed to purple and hyacinth <note>These colors refer to the "tekhelet" (blue/violet) and "argaman" (purple) used in the biblical Tabernacle and the Temple of Solomon, symbolizing royalty and the heavens.</note>.
**Q.** What is this radiance, this brilliant light?
**A.** The *Shekinah* <note>original: *Stékenna*. A transliteration of the Hebrew word meaning "dwelling" or "settling," referring to the divine presence or glory of God inhabiting the Temple.</note> and the glory of the Grand Architect.
**Q.** What was the purpose of this proclamation?
**A.** The recognition that all the Brothers made of my person to replace Adonhiram, declaring me Superintendent of the works of the Temple by naming me *Moabon* <note>A ritual name used in various Masonic degrees; in this context, it signifies the candidate's new identity and elevation to high office.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the initiation narrative for the Scottish Master, where the candidate transitions from mourning the death of Adonhiram to being proclaimed his successor. It highlights the use of symbolic ritual words and the appearance of the divine presence (Shekinah) within the Temple.</summary>
<keywords>Adonhiram, Civi, Caki, Shekinah, Grand Architect, Moabon, Superintendent, Temple of Solomon, Scottish Master</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the catechism of the Scottish Master degree. Following the description of the candidate's transition from the mourning of Adonhiram to the light of the "Shekinah," the text now provides a symbolic explanation of the degree’s jewel and its sacred geography.</meta>

**Q.** What does the luminous triangle represent?
**A.** The glory of the Great Architect of the Universe <note>The "Grand Architect of the Universe" (GAOTU) is a neutral term used in Masonry to represent the Supreme Being, allowing members of different faiths to join in common ritual.</note>, and the three divine properties: eternity, omniscience <note>original: *science*. In this 18th-century context, it refers to all-encompassing divine knowledge.</note>, and power.

**Q.** What is the meaning of the letters within it?
**A.** The name of God, the sacred word of the Scottish Masters <note>These letters typically represent the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (YHWH), the unpronounceable name of God.</note>. This demonstrates to us that God is the source and the center of all light, and that He alone can know all things.

**Q.** Why do the Scottish Masters wear the triangle?
**A.** Because it is the emblem of the triple unity.

**Q.** What does the circle surrounding it represent?
**A.** That God had no beginning, and that He shall have no end.

**Q.** Why is this jewel suspended from a fire-colored ribbon?
**A.** To teach us the ardor <note>original: *ardeur*. This implies a burning zeal or spiritual passion.</note> we must employ to strike down vices.

**Q.** In what place did you hold your first Lodge?
**A.** Between three mountains inaccessible to the Profane <note>original: *Profanes*. A Masonic term for those who have not been initiated into the order; literally those "outside the temple."</note>, in a deep valley where peace, virtue, and union reign.

**Q.** What do you name these three mountains?

<summary>This page details the symbolic significance of the Scottish Master's jewel, specifically the luminous triangle and circle representing the divine nature, and introduces the allegorical setting of the degree's first lodge.</summary>
<keywords>Great Architect of the Universe, luminous triangle, Scottish Master, sacred word, divine attributes, Masonic jewel, Profane, symbolic mountains</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the catechism of the Scottish Master degree. The previous page ended with a question about the names of three mountains where the first Lodge was held. This page identifies those mountains and explains the symbolic geography and secret modes of recognition associated with the grade.</meta>

**A.** Mount Moriah, within the enclosure of the grounds of Gibeon <note>original: *Gabaon*. In biblical tradition, Gibeon was a high place where Solomon offered sacrifices before the Temple was built on Mount Moriah.</note>, Mount Sinai, and the mountain of Heredon <note>Heredon (or Harodim) is a mythical mountain in Masonic lore, often associated with Scotland and the origins of the "higher" degrees.</note>.

**Q.** What is this mountain of Heredon?

**A.** A mountain situated between the West and the North of Scotland, at the end of the sun’s course, where the first Lodge of Masonry was held, in that earthly region which gave its name to Scottish Masonry <note>original: *Maçonnerie Ecossoise*. This refers to the "Ecossais" systems of Masonry which became highly popular in 18th-century France, claiming a lineage back to Scotland.</note>.

**Q.** What do you mean by "deep valley"?

**A.** I mean the tranquility of our Lodges.

**Q.** What can produce this tranquility?

**A.** The preservation of our marks since their origin.

**Q.** What are the marks of the Scottish Master?

**A.** Two signs, a grip <note>original: *attouchement*. A specific physical gesture or handshake used by Masons to recognize one another's rank in secret.</note>, and four words, one of which is incommunicable <note>original: *incommunicable*. This refers to a sacred name or word that is never spoken aloud or only whispered under specific ritual conditions.</note>.

**Q.** Give me the sign?

(*In response, the sign is performed.*)

**Q.** Give the grip to the Second Brother <note>original: *Frere second*. This likely refers to the Junior Warden or the second highest-ranking officer present in the ritual.</note>?

(*In response, the grip is given.*)

**Q.** How do you name this grip?

**A.** The perfect trial <note>original: *parfaite épreuve*. This can also be translated as "the perfect proof," signifying that the grip confirms the member's identity and rank beyond doubt.</note>.

**Q.** Give me the incommunicable word?

<meta>This page concludes the catechism of the Scottish Master degree (the sixth grade in this specific rite). It transitions from the secret words of the degree to a symbolic explanation of the Lodge's purification and a list of ritual ages for each Masonic rank.</meta>

**A.** I will spell it with you. <note>In many Masonic degrees, sacred or secret words are not spoken in full but are spelled letter-by-letter between the two speakers.</note>

**Q.** What does this word mean?

**A.** The name of the Grand Architect.

**Q.** Give the first two words?

*(The response is given.)*

**Q.** Give me the password?

**A.** Zedidiac. <note>This is a ritual word likely derived from the Hebrew "Tzedakah" (righteousness) or "Zadok" (the High Priest), common in the high-degree Masonry of the 18th century.</note>

**Q.** Why is the Lodge purified before work?

**A.** Because after the construction of the Temple, the Grand Architect caused fire to descend from heaven to purify it and consume the burnt offering. <note>original: *holocauste*. This refers to the biblical account in 2 Chronicles 7:1, where divine fire marks the consecration of Solomon’s Temple.</note>

**Q.** How old are you? <note>In Masonry, "age" is a symbolic representation of one's progress and the time spent in study within the fraternity, rather than chronological years.</note>

**A.** As an Apprentice, 3 years; as a Fellowcraft, 5; as a Master, 9; as a Minor Architect, 21; as a Grand Architect, 27; as a Scot, 81. <note>original: *Ecossois*. The age 81 (9x9) is highly symbolic in numerology, representing the ultimate perfection of the Scottish Master degree.</note>

**This Lodge is closed in the same manner as the previous one.**

->End of the Sixth Grade.<-

# THE KNIGHT OF THE SWORD.
### SURNAMED
# KNIGHT OF THE EAST
## OR OF THE EAGLE.

---

->*Arrangement and decoration of the Lodge.*<-

**THIS** Grade requires two rooms on the same level. The first must be hung with green; but it must be noted that this hanging must be thick and attached to the ceiling on three sides, which are the West, the North, and the East, in such a way that there is about six feet of space between it and the wall. The space enclosed within the hanging must be a "long square" <note>original: *carré long*. This is a traditional Masonic term for a rectangle, symbolizing the shape of the Temple and the world.</note>. It represents the apartment of Cyrus, King of the Assyrians <note>Cyrus the Great was historically King of the Persians; however, 18th-century Masonic rituals often used "Assyrians" or "Babylonians" to refer generally to the Eastern empires of the biblical era.</note>. It must be illuminated by 70 lights, to mark the 70 years of captivity. In the East, there must be a Throne; in the West, two armchairs; and in the South, seats for the Brethren. Behind the Throne, there must be a transparency <note>A "transparency" was a painting on translucent cloth or paper, illuminated from behind to create a glowing effect, frequently used in 18th-century ceremonies.</note>, representing the dream of Cyrus; namely, a lion...

In this context, it likely refers to the Tetragrammaton—the four-letter Hebrew name of God—written inside a radiant triangle or sunburst, a common symbol in Masonic lodges.</note>. This glory is carried by a luminous cloud, from which emerges an Eagle carrying this motto in its beak: *Restore liberty to the Captives.* And below, one sees Nebuchadnezzar and Belshazzar <note>original: *Balthazar*. In early Freemasonry, the "tableau" or "tracing board" was a carpet or board placed in the center of the room depicting the symbols of the degree. This is traditional Masonic terminology for a rectangle.</note>, formed by a sort of small wall of wood or painted cardboard, about one and a half feet high.

This small wall begins at the two sides of the Throne, passes at the feet of the Brothers in the South, reaches as far as the hanging in the West—so that the two armchairs of which I spoke are inside the square—and continues along the hanging in the North as far as the East. At the four corners of this wall, as well as in the middle of the North and the South, there must be a small tower that exceeds the height of the wall by one and a half feet. There must be a seventh tower in the West which divides the wall in two, as well as the hanging. This tower must be seven feet high, and its circumference must be proportioned so that a man can stand in it easily. There must be two doors to this tower; one inside the Lodge, and the other outside. To

<meta>This page continues the physical description of the ritual lodge for the Knight of the East degree, moving from the construction of the symbolic wall of Babylon (described on the previous page) to the passage over a bridge and into the second chamber representing Jerusalem.</meta>

Near this latter, there must be two sentinels armed with a pike and a sword, who are consequently located within the six feet of space to the West. In the remaining space that continues through the North until behind the East—where the door to the second chamber is located—there must be a solid bridge, illuminated by a lantern. The entrance to this bridge must be guarded by several armed men, and the other end should terminate near the door of the second chamber. Upon the bridge, there must be water arranged in such a way that it can be agitated, representing the river Staburzanaï <note>original: *Staburzanaï*. In Masonic tradition, this name refers to a river the captives must cross to return to Jerusalem. It is likely a corruption of the name *Shethar-bozenai*, a Persian official mentioned in the Book of Ezra.</note>.

### Second Chamber.

This chamber represents the enclosure in which the Temple stood. The hangings <note>Masonic "hangings" were fabric coverings used to decorate the walls of a meeting room according to the theme of the degree being conferred.</note> must be red. The Floor Cloth <term>Tableau: A symbolic painting or "tracing board" depicting the emblems of the degree, usually placed in the center of the room.</term> is the same as in the Master Scot <note>original: *Maître Ecossois*. This refers to an earlier degree in the Masonic system; the use of the same imagery suggests a thematic link between the "Scottish" degrees and the rebuilding of the Temple.</note> degree. Additionally, one can see a corner of the entrance to the Temple, where the broken pillar Boaz <note>original: *Booz*. Boaz was one of the two bronze pillars at the entrance of Solomon's Temple. Its "broken" state here symbolizes the ruins of the first Temple following the Babylonian conquest.</note> is visible. This Floor Cloth must be covered with a red cloth; and we shall see later the moment when it must be uncovered.

The Master represents Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great, the Persian King who liberated the Jews from the Babylonian Captivity.</note> and is called...

They also have a white apron lined with green taffeta, bordered with a small ribbon of the same color, without any other markings of Freemasonry.

### Titles, Ornaments, and Jewels of the Second Chamber.

In this chamber, the Master is called Most Excellent; the Wardens, Most Puissant; the Brothers, Most Venerable; and the Candidate, Zerubbabel <note>Zerubbabel was the leader of the Jewish people who returned from the Babylonian Captivity to rebuild the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>. When passing from the first chamber into this one, one leaves the green behind to take up the red. However, the various Degrees are distinguished by rosettes located at the bottom of the sash, placed one on top of the other: namely, a blue one for the Junior Architect <note>original: *Petit Architecte*</note>, a poppy-red <note>original: *ponceau*. This refers to a deep, bright red color similar to that of a wild poppy.</note> one for the Grand Architect, and a red one for...

<meta>This page continues the description of the regalia for the higher degrees. ...the Scottish <note>referring to the Scottish Master degree</note>, a green one for the Knight of the East, and a black one for the Knight of the Eagle. The Brothers wear a sash of water-colored <note>likely "watered silk" or moiré</note> silk, bordered with a gold fringe, and scattered with images of skulls, crossbones arranged in a saltire <term>sautoir: a cross in the shape of an X</term>, and gold triangular chains. In the middle, it is crossed by a gold band representing a bridge, upon which are three letters: L. D. P. <note>original: "L. D. P." These letters traditionally stand for *Liberté de Passer* (Freedom of Passage), referencing the Jews' return across the river to Jerusalem, though some interpretations suggest the Latin *Lilia Destrue Pedibus* (Trample the lilies underfoot).</note> This sash is worn around the body as a belt, in such a way that the ends, trimmed with gold fringe, hang over the tails of the coat.

The Master and the Officers wear their jewels around the neck, and the Brothers wear theirs at the bottom of their sash. The Master’s jewel consists of three triangles nested one inside the other in a gradient. The Senior Warden wears the square, and the Junior Warden wears the level; all the Officers wear their usual jewels, but enclosed within a triple triangle. The shape of the jewel is the same as that used by the Scottish Masters. Furthermore, there must be two swords with their blades crossed in a saltire and their hilts resting on the level. Everything must be gold or gilded. All the Brothers must have a trowel hanging from the belt of their apron.

### *Opening of the Lodge.*

*The Sovereign.* » My Brothers, help me to open the Lodge of the Knight of the Sword«.
The Generals <note>In this degree, the presiding officers are titled "Sovereign" and "Generals" rather than "Master" and "Wardens."</note> repeat this. The Sovereign strikes seven blows, with a pause between the 5th and 6th; and the Generals do the same. The Sovereign says: » First General, examine whether we are in safety, and if all the Brothers are worthy Knights «.
The General obeys, and says afterward: « Sovereign Master, we are sheltered from the Uninitiated <term>profanes: those who have not been initiated into the mysteries of Masonry or this specific degree</term>, and all the Brothers present are Knights of the Sword «.
The Sovereign asks: » What time is it «?
The first General answers: » The day

<meta>This page continues the ritual dialogue for the opening of the Lodge in the Knight of the Sword degree. The previous page ended with the First General stating that the "day" of the prophecy regarding the 70 years of captivity was nearing its fulfillment.</meta>

...of the 70 years of captivity is accomplished <note>This refers to the Babylonian Captivity of the Jewish people, which according to biblical prophecy was to last seventy years before their return to Jerusalem.</note>.

*The Sovereign.* » Generals, Princes, Knights, it has been a long time since I resolved to set free the Jews who are captive. I am weary of seeing them groan in chains; but I cannot deliver them without consulting you on a dream I had tonight, which requires explanation. I thought I saw a roaring Lion ready to throw itself upon me to devour me. Its appearance terrified me and made me flee to seek a refuge against its fury; but at that moment I perceived my predecessors, who served as a footstool to a glory that the Masons <term>Maçons: the members of the fraternity</term> designate by the name of Grand Architects of the Universe <note>original: "Grands Architectes de l'Univers." Interestingly, the French text uses the plural "Architects" here, though the concept is usually singular in modern Freemasonry.</note>. Two words were heard; they came from the center of the luminous star. I discerned that they meant to grant liberty to the Captives, or else my crown would pass into foreign hands. I remained stunned and confused. The dream disappeared. Since that moment my peace is lost; it is for you, Princes, to help me with your advice to deliberate on what I must do. «

During this speech the Brothers all have their heads bowed; but at the end they look at the first General, imitating him.

**The Sovereign.** "Let the captivity end. Generals, Princes, Knights, the Lodge of the Knights of the Sword is open."
The Generals repeat the same thing, each on his side. The Sovereign and all the Brothers perform the usual acclamations, but without applause <note>In many Masonic rites, "acclamations" are specific rhythmic cries or gestures, distinct from common clapping or applause.</note>.

# I N I T I A T I O N <note>original: "RÉCEPTION." In this context, it refers to the formal ceremony of admitting a candidate into this specific degree of the order.</note>

When the Candidate <term>Récipiendaire: the person being initiated</term> is in the proper state, the Master of Ceremonies <term>Maître des Cérémonies: the officer who directs the movements and floorwork of the ritual</term> leads him to the door of the tower, to the Guards, as mentioned above. The Guards question him. He must repeat what the Master of Ceremonies tells him.
**The Guard.** *Q.* What do you ask?
*A.* I ask if it is possible to speak to your Sovereign.
**The Guard.** *Q.* Who are you?
*A.* The first among my equals, a Mason <term>Maçon: a member of the fraternity, here specifically referring to the historical builders of the Temple</term> by rank, a Captive by misfortune <note>original: "disgrace." This refers to the biblical narrative of the Jewish people's exile in Babylon.</note>.
*Q.* What is your name?

**Response.** Zerubbabel <note>A biblical figure who, according to tradition, led the first group of Jews back from the Babylonian Captivity and laid the foundation for the Second Temple.</note>.
**Question.** What is your age?
**Response.** 70 years <note>This represents the 70 years of the Babylonian Captivity as prophesied by Jeremiah.</note>.
**Question.** What is the matter that brings you here?
**Response.** The tears and the misery of my Brothers.
**The Guard.** "Wait. We will try to convey your grievances to the Sovereign."
One of the Guards knocks seven times at the door of the tower as a Knight of the Sword <note>Masonic rituals often use specific patterns of knocks, known as "batteries," to identify members of a particular degree.</note>.
The second General knocks seven times with the mallet of the first; then the Sovereign.
**The second General.** "A Guard knocks at the door of the tower as a Knight of the Sword."
**The first General.** "Sovereign Master, a Guard knocks at the door of the tower as a Knight of the Sword."
**The Sovereign.** "First General, let him be introduced. Guard me with extraordinary precautions. In the state of distress I am in, there is no small warning to be neglected."
The second General goes to the door of the tower, knocks, opens, and leads the Guard back to the West <note>In Masonic symbolism, the West is often the location of the entrance or the station of certain officers.</note>. The Guard sets down his pike <term>Pique: a long polearm weapon used by infantry</term>, crosses his arms, bows, and says: "The first among the Masons, his equals, aged 70 years, asks to appear before you."

The Sovereign. "Let him be introduced into the palace tower; we shall question him."
The Guard makes another bow, withdraws, and has the Recipient <note>The "Recipient" is the candidate being initiated into the degree.</note> enter the tower, then closes it again. Then the Sovereign asks the Recipient, through the door which must be closed:
**Q.** What matter brings you here?
**R.** I come to implore the justice and the goodness of the Sovereign.
**Q.** Concerning what?
**R.** To ask mercy for my Brothers who have been in servitude for 70 years.
**Q.** What is your name?
**R.** Zerubbabel, the first among my equals, a Mason by rank, a Captive by misfortune <note>original: "disgrace"</note>.
**Q.** What favor do you have to ask of me?
**R.** That under the favor of the Grand Architect of the Universe <term>Grand Architect of the Universe: A deistic term used in Freemasonry to refer to God, allowing men of different faiths to join in common prayer</term>, the King’s justice may grant us liberty, and that he may permit us to go and rebuild the Temple of our God.
The Sovereign. "Since such just motives bring him here, let the freedom to appear before us with his face uncovered <note>In many Masonic rituals, candidates are blindfolded or "hoodwinked." This command signifies the removal of the blindfold or the granting of a formal audience.</note> be granted to him." Immediately the Guards go to open the tower door, lead him to the West <note>In a Masonic lodge, the "West" typically refers to the area near the entrance, opposite the Master's chair in the East.</note>, and make him prostrate himself.
The Sovereign. "Zerubbabel, I have felt, as you have, the weight of your captivity."

The Sovereign. "I am ready to deliver them to you, by granting you freedom this instant, if you wish to communicate to me the secrets of Masonry, for which I have always had the deepest veneration."

The Recipient. "Sovereign Master, when Solomon <note>King Solomon, the biblical builder of the First Temple and a central figure in Masonic tradition.</note> gave us the first principles, he taught us that equality must be the first motive. It does not reign here. Your rank, your titles, your superiority, and your Court are not compatible with the place where one is instructed in the mysteries of our Order. Furthermore, our external signs <term>external signs: Secret gestures, handshakes, and passwords used by Masons to recognize one another.</term> are unknown to you. My engagements are inviolable, and I cannot reveal our secrets to you. If my liberty is at this price, I prefer captivity."

The Sovereign. "I admire the discretion and the virtue of Zerubbabel; he deserves liberty for his firmness in his engagements."

The Brothers all acquiesce <note>The "Brothers" represent the members of the lodge acting as the King's court.</note> by lowering the points of their swords and then raising them again.

The Sovereign. "Second General, have Zerubbabel undergo the 70 trials, which I reduce to three; namely, the trial of the body, that of the mind, and that of the soul, so that through these he may deserve the grace he asks, and that his discretion encourages me to grant it to him."

<summary>In this scene of the ritual, Zerubbabel refuses to betray the secrets of Masonry to King Cyrus even in exchange for his freedom. Impressed by this integrity, the King orders that he undergo three symbolic trials to prove his worthiness.</summary>
<keywords>Zerubbabel, King Cyrus, Masonic secrets, Solomon, equality, initiation trials, Babylonian captivity, Knight of the Sword</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the initiation ritual for the Knight of the Sword (or of the East). Following Zerubbabel’s refusal to betray Masonic secrets in exchange for his freedom, King Cyrus has ordered him to undergo three symbolic trials to prove his worthiness.</meta>

( 113 )

The second General has him walk three times around the Lodge <note>In this ritual context, the "Lodge" represents the royal court of King Cyrus in Babylon.</note>. At the first turn, a firecracker is set off <note>A "pétard" or firecracker was used in these ceremonies to create a sudden, loud explosion, testing the candidate's composure during the "trial by fire" or noise.</note>; at the second, he is asked if he persists in demanding liberty; at the third, he is made to place both hands over his forehead. Upon returning, the second General strikes seven times, and the first General asks him: "What do you demand?"

The second General. "The Candidate has undergone the trials with firmness and constancy."

The Sovereign. "I grant you, Zerubbabel <note>Zerubbabel is the biblical figure who led the Jewish people back from the Babylonian Captivity to rebuild the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>, the favor that you ask of me; I consent to your being set free."

The Sovereign strikes seven times, which serves as a signal for the Generals to remove Zerubbabel’s chains; this they do instantly. Then the Sovereign says:

> "Go into your country; I permit you to rebuild the Temple destroyed by my ancestors; let your treasures be returned to you before the sun has set. Be recognized as a leader over your equals. I shall order that you be obeyed in every place through which you pass, and that you be given aid and assistance as if it were to myself. I require of you only a simple tribute <term>tribute: A symbolic tax or gift paid by a protected ruler to a sovereign as a sign of respect and alliance.</term> of three lambs, five sheep, and seven rams, which I shall send someone to receive under the portico of the new Temple. If I ask for this, it is more to remember the friendship..."

# I N S T R U C T I O N

**Q.** <note>original: "D." for Demande (Question).</note> Brother Senior Warden <term>Surveillant: an officer of the Lodge responsible for supervising the members and assisting the presiding Master.</term>, how were you admitted to the eminent Grade of Knight of the Sword?
**A.** <note>original: "R." for Réponse (Answer).</note> I arrived there through humility, patience, and frequent solicitations.

<summary>This page concludes the ceremonial installation of the candidate into the role of Zerubbabel and initiates the "Instruction," a traditional question-and-answer catechism used to teach the degree's secrets and history.</summary>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional catechism of the Knight of the East (or Knight of the Sword) degree. Having been symbolically "reborn" as Zerubbabel, the candidate now undergoes a formal series of questions and answers that explain the historical and moral foundations of the degree, focusing on the return from Babylonian exile.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>

Q. To whom did you address yourself?
A. To the great King. <note>This refers to Cyrus the Great, the King of Persia, who historically permitted the Jewish people to return to Jerusalem.</note>
Q. What is your name?
A. Zerubbabel.
Q. Your country?
A. Judea. I was born of noble parents from the Tribe of Judah.
Q. What art do you profess?
A. Masonry.
Q. What buildings do you build?
A. Temples and Tabernacles.
Q. Where do you build them?
A. For want of land <note>original: "faute de terrain" — This refers to the period of exile when the Jewish people had no physical territory of their own, necessitating a spiritual form of worship.</note>, we build them in our hearts.
Q. What is the name of a Knight Mason?
A. That of a very free Mason.
Q. Why very free?
A. Because the Masons who were chosen by Solomon to work on the Temple <note>The First Temple, or Solomon's Temple, destroyed by the Babylonians in 586 BCE.</note> were declared free and exempt from all taxes, for themselves and their descendants. They also had the privilege of carrying arms. Upon the destruction of the Temple by Nebuchadnezzar <note>The King of Babylon who conquered Jerusalem.</note>, they were taken into captivity with the Jewish people; but the kindness of King Cyrus gave them permission to build a second Temple under Zerubbabel, and restored their liberty. It is from this era that we bear the name of Free Masons. <note>This provides a "legendary history" for the term "Freemason," linking the modern fraternity to the biblical account of liberation from slavery.</note>

<summary>This page contains a formal catechism explaining the identity of the Knight Mason as Zerubbabel and establishing the legendary origins of "Free Masonry" as a status granted by King Solomon and restored by King Cyrus.</summary>
<keywords>Zerubbabel, Tribe of Judah, Solomon, Nebuchadnezzar, King Cyrus, Free Masons, Second Temple, Babylonian Captivity, symbolic building</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional catechism for the Knight of the East degree, following the candidate's symbolic identification as Zerubbabel. The focus shifts from the personal history of the captive Jews to the architectural and mystical origins of the first Temple of Solomon.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
<sig>L 3</sig>

Q. Was the ancient Temple beautiful?
A. It was the first wonder of the world in richness and grandeur; for its courtyard could contain two hundred thousand people.
Q. Who was the principal Architect who built this great edifice?
A. God was the first, Solomon the second, and Adonhiram the third. <note>In Masonic tradition, Adonhiram is often identified as the overseer of the craftsmen and a key figure in the construction of the Temple, sometimes distinguished from or substituted for Hiram Abiff depending on the specific rite.</note>
Q. Who laid the first stone?
A. Solomon.
Q. At what hour was it laid?
A. Before sunrise.
Q. Why?
A. To demonstrate the vigilance that we must have for the service of the <term>Architect of the Universe</term> <note>A universalist title used in Masonry to refer to the Divine, allowing members of different faiths to unite in a shared moral framework.</note>.
Q. What cement was used there?
A. A mystical cement, composed of flour, milk, oil, and wine.
Q. Explain the mystical meaning to me?
A. To form the first man, the <term>Supreme Being</term> employed sweetness, wisdom, strength, and goodness. <note>This symbolic "cement" links the physical construction of the Temple to the creation of humanity; the flour, milk, oil, and wine are allegories for the virtues that bind a person's character together.</note>
Q. Where was the first stone laid?
A. In the middle of the room intended for the Sanctuary.
Q. How many doors did the ancient Temple have?
A. Three: one to the West, one to the South, and one to the North. <note>The absence of a door to the East is significant in Masonic ritual, often representing the place where the Sun (or Divine Light) rises, which needs no physical door to enter the sacred space.</note>

<summary>This page presents a Masonic catechism detailing the construction of Solomon’s Temple, emphasizing the symbolic "mystical cement" and the divine role in its architecture. It links the physical act of building to the moral development of the individual and the creation of humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Temple of Solomon, Adonhiram, Architect of the Universe, mystical cement, Supreme Being, Sanctuary, Masonic ritual, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the catechism for the Knight of the East degree. While the previous page focused on the construction and mystical foundations of Solomon’s Temple, this section transitions to its destruction under the Babylonians and the symbolic lessons derived from the Jewish captivity.</meta>

<page-num>( 126 )</page-num>

Q. How long did the Temple last?
A. 470 years, 6 months, and 10 days.
Q. Under which King of Israel was it destroyed?
A. Under the reign of <term>Zedekiah</term> <note>Original: "Sédécias." Zedekiah was the last king of Judah before the destruction of Jerusalem by Nebuchadnezzar II.</note>, the last of the line of David.
Q. What is the meaning of the broken <term>Boaz</term> column? <note>Boaz and Jachin were the two great copper pillars at the porch of Solomon’s Temple. In Masonic symbolism, a broken column often represents the fall of the Temple or the death of a significant figure.</note>
A. The confusion and evil committed when one admits someone who is not worthy.
Q. Why is the number 81 held in such veneration among Masons?
A. Because this number explains the triple essence of the Divinity, represented by the triple triangle, by the square of 9, and the number 3. <note>In this mathematical symbolism, 3 is the root; 3 multiplied by 3 is 9 (the square); and 9 multiplied by 9 is 81. This "triple-triple" progression represents the reaching of divine perfection.</note>
Q. Why are the chains of the Captives triangular?
A. The Assyrians, having learned that the triangle was for them the emblem of the name of the <term>Eternal</term> <note>A common Masonic and period reference to God.</note>, fashioned the chains in this shape to cause more sorrow to the Captives. <note>This suggests a psychological cruelty: forcing the captives to wear their most sacred symbol as a physical restraint.</note>
Q. Why was it forbidden for Masons to work on <term>profane</term> buildings? <note>In Masonry, "profane" simply refers to anything outside the temple or the craft—the secular world.</note>
A. To teach us not to frequent irregular Lodges.
Q. What was the plan that <term>Cyrus</term> gave for the new Temple? <note>Cyrus the Great, King of Persia, who allowed the Jews to return to Jerusalem to rebuild the Temple.</note>
A. 100 <term>cubits</term> in depth, 60 in width, and the same in height. <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of measure based on the length of the forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the historical and symbolic timeline of the Temple's destruction and the Babylonian captivity, using mathematical and architectural allegories to teach lessons on worthiness and devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Zedekiah, Boaz, number 81, triangular chains, irregular lodges, Cyrus the Great, cubits, Temple of Solomon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the ritual catechism for the Knight of the East or Knight of the Sword degree. While the previous page discussed the destruction of the first Temple and the hardships of the Babylonian captivity, this section focuses on the decree of Cyrus the Great and the symbolic tools used during the reconstruction of the Second Temple.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

Q. Why did Cyrus order that the wood be cut from the forests of Lebanon, and the stones be drawn from the quarries of Tyre for the construction of the new Temple?
A. Because it was necessary that the second Temple be in every way similar to the first. <note>Solomon’s original Temple was also built using cedars from Lebanon and stone from the Phoenician city of Tyre, symbolizing a restoration of the original divine plan.</note>

Q. Give me the name of the principal Architect who directed the work of the second Temple.
A. <term>Bibot</term> is his name. <note>In various versions of Masonic ritual, "Bibot" (sometimes Libodot or Bebe) is identified as a master architect or foreman assisting Zerubbabel during the return from exile.</note>

Q. Why the sword that the Workmen carry while working?
A. It is because while they worked with one hand to carry materials and rebuild the Temple, as they were subject to the raids of their enemies, they held their swords ready to defend their work and their Brothers. <note>This "sword and trowel" imagery is a central theme of this degree, drawn from the biblical Book of Nehemiah, representing the dual duty of the Mason to build the community and defend the truth.</note>

Q. Why the 70 lights in the Lodge?
A. In memory of the 70 years of the <term>Babylonian captivity</term>.

Q. Are you a <term>Knight of the Sword</term>?
A. Look at me. (He takes his sword in hand.)

Q. Give me the sign?
->( In response, he performs it. )<-

Q. Give me the word and the password?
A. Judah, Benjamin, and Liberty <note>Original: "Juda, Benjamin et Libertas." Judah and Benjamin were the two tribes of the Southern Kingdom who returned to Jerusalem; "Libertas" is Latin for liberty, referring to the freedom granted to the captives by Cyrus.</note>.

<summary>This page details the symbolic reconstruction of the Second Temple, emphasizing the "sword and trowel" motif where Masons must simultaneously build and defend. It concludes with the specific passwords—Judah, Benjamin, and Liberty—marking the end of the Babylonian exile.</summary>

<keywords>Second Temple, Cyrus the Great, Bibot, Lebanon, Tyre, sword and trowel, Babylonian captivity, Judah, Benjamin, Libertas, Knight of the Sword</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page concludes the ritual for the Knight of the Sword (or Knight of the East), the sixth degree. It follows the catechism regarding the reconstruction of the Temple of Jerusalem under Zerubbabel and moves into the formal closing of the Lodge.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>

**Q.** Give the grip <note>The "attouchement" or grip is a specific handshake used as a secret sign of recognition between members of this degree.</note> to the <term>Senior Warden</term>? <note>The Senior Warden (Premier Surveillant) is the second-in-command of the Lodge, typically situated in the West.</note>

*( In response, he gives it. )*

**Q.** Where have you worked?
**A.** At the rebuilding of the second Temple. <note>Referring to the Temple built by the Jews returning from the Babylonian exile, led by Zerubbabel, as described in the Book of Ezra.</note>
**Q.** What time is it?
**A.** The moment of the rebuilding.

The Master says: My Brothers, since we are fortunate enough to have rebuilt the Temple of the Lord in its splendor, let us preserve its memory and its signs through our silence: it is time for us to rest. Brother Senior and Junior Wardens, announce, on both the South and North sides, that I am going to close the <term>Lodge of the Knights of the Sword</term>. The two Wardens announce, each on his side, that the Master is going to close the Lodge; then the <term>Most Excellent</term> <note>The title "Très-Excellent" (Most Excellent) refers to the presiding Master of the Lodge in this specific degree, representing King Cyrus or Zerubbabel.</note> strikes seven knocks, and the two Wardens do the same; then the Master says: The Lodge is closed, everyone is permitted to withdraw. The Wardens repeat this. The usual applause and acclamations are performed. <note>In 18th-century French Masonry, "acclamations" often involved shouting "Vivat!" or other rhythmic cheers accompanied by specific patterns of hand-clapping.</note>

->**End of the sixth Degree.**<-

# THE KNIGHT ROSE-CROIX.

---

### *Titles and Jewels.*

The Lodge is named the <term>Sovereign Chapter</term> <note>A "Chapter" is the specific name for a body of Masons meeting in the Rose-Croix degree, distinct from a "Lodge" used for the first three degrees.</note>. The Master is called <term>Most Wise</term> <note>original: "Très-Sage". This title reflects the philosophical and spiritual wisdom associated with this degree.</note>. The <term>Wardens</term> <note>Officers who assist the Master; the "Surveillans" are responsible for order and the instruction of the members.</note> are named Most Respectable First and Second Knight Wardens. All the other Officers, who are an Orator, a Secretary, a Master of Ceremonies, etc., are likewise named Most Respectable Knights, as are all the Brothers. The former are distinguished only by the name of their office. The <term>Jewel</term> <note>The "Bijou" is the symbolic medal or ornament worn by a Mason to indicate his rank or degree.</note> is a kind of triangle formed with a compass and a <term>quadrant</term> <note>original: "quart-de-cercle". A tool representing a 90-degree arc, used for measurement and symbolic of rectitude.</note>. In the middle is a Cross upon which is a Rose, and at the bottom, upon the quadrant, is a <term>Pelican</term> bleeding itself for its young <note>The "Pelican in its Piety" is a traditional symbol of self-sacrifice and charity, based on the legend that the bird would pierce its own breast to feed its starving chicks with its blood; in this context, it represents the Redeemer or the ideal of selfless love.</note>. This Jewel is attached to a black rosette, and hangs from a wide, watered red ribbon <note>A "moiré" or watered ribbon has a wavy, shimmering pattern.</note>, worn around the neck, or as a <term>sash</term> <note>original: "en sautoir". This refers to a ribbon worn either as a collar or across the chest.</note>. This Jewel is worn thus in all Lodges, except during the <term>first point</term> <note>Masonic rituals of this era were often divided into "points" or sections. The "first point" of the Rose-Croix ritual is mournful and somber, representing a world in darkness.</note> of the Rose-Croix, where one is obliged to wear the black Ribbon.

<vocab>Rose-Croix, Sovereign Chapter, Most Wise, Wardens, Jewel, Pelican, Ribbon</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the Knight Rose-Croix degree, outlining the specific titles used for its officers and describing the symbolic jewel, which features a cross, a rose, and a pelican. It also notes the requirement to wear a black ribbon during the first section of the ritual, signifying a period of mourning or darkness.</summary>
<keywords>Knight Rose-Croix, Sovereign Chapter, Most Wise, Pelican in its Piety, Masonic symbolism, degree regalia, 18th-century masonry</keywords>

### *Observations on this Degree and its Reception.*

Although this Degree is the ultimate peak <note>original: "*nec plus ultrà*"; a Latin phrase meaning "nothing further beyond," signifying the highest possible attainment.</note> of <term>Masonry</term>, and that once one possesses it, one has the right to attend all other <term>Lodges</term> without undergoing examination, there is nonetheless no Lodge where the participants are less under suspicion than in these; the reason being that no <term>Brother</term> is admitted unless he is well known, or unless he holds a <term>Brief</term> ( 1 ), signed by all those who attended his reception. No <term>candidate</term> <note>original: "prosélyte"; here referring to a Mason seeking to advance to a higher degree.</note> is accepted unless he is of an honorable status and his conduct is irreproachable. It is also an absolute necessity that he has passed through the <term>Degrees of Elected, Scots, and Knight of the East</term> <note>These represent the progression of the "High Degrees" (Hauts Grades) that preceded the Rose-Croix in the 18th-century rite.</note>. There are three "points" <note>Significant parts or ceremonial stages of the ritual.</note> in the Rose-Croix: two for the reception, and another named the <term>Mystic Supper</term> <note>original: "Cene mystique"; a ritual meal of bread and wine shared among the members, emphasizing fraternal unity.</note>, which is used only four times a year, or on the solemn feast days of the Order.

---

( 1 ) A certificate that one is obliged to give to every Brother after his reception.

### *Decoration and layout of the <term>Lodge</term>.*

The <term>Lodge</term> <note>While the assembly is called a Chapter, the room itself is often still referred to as the Lodge.</note> must be draped in red; and in the <term>East</term>, instead of a Throne, there is a triangular Altar, one face of which is turned toward the <term>West</term>. This Altar must be raised upon seven steps. Upon this Altar, there must be a large transparent painting <note>original: "Tableau en transparent"; these were paintings on thin fabric or paper, illuminated from behind by candles to create a glowing, ethereal effect in a darkened room.</note>, representing Calvary. The two Crosses on the sides are bare; but on the one in the middle, there is a rose and an interlaced drapery; and above it, the inscription that was on the Cross of the Savior <note>This refers to the "I.N.R.I." (Iesus Nazarenus Rex Iudaeorum), which in this degree is often given a specific Masonic interpretation: *Igne Natura Renovatur Integra* (By fire, nature is restored whole).</note>. At the bottom, on the front of the painting, there are broken columns, upon the ruins of which are sleeping Guards; and in their midst, one sees a kind of tomb, the top stone of which is displaced, and from which a shroud emerges. There are also three triangular Tables or Altars required; upon each of them, a light. These Tables are placed one to the right of the East, at the foot of the steps, and the two others in the West, opposite each <term>Warden</term> <note>original: "Surveillant"; one of the principal officers who helps govern the assembly.</note>. All of this decoration is used whenever a <term>Chapter</term> <note>A "Chapter" is the specific name for a meeting of the Rose-Croix degree.</note> is held. But when there is a <term>Reception</term> <note>An initiation ceremony for a new candidate.</note>, this must only be in the second point <note>A specific stage or "act" of the ritual.</note>, given that, in the fir— <note>The sentence ends abruptly here and continues on the following page; it likely refers to the "first point" of the ceremony having a different, more somber decoration.</note>

<summary>This page describes the ritual environment for the Rose-Croix degree, detailing a red-draped room centered on a glowing depiction of Calvary, broken columns, and an empty tomb. It specifies the arrangement of triangular altars for the officers and notes that this specific layout is used for regular meetings and the second stage of initiations.</summary>

[first], the entire hangings, the transparency <note>original: "le transparent"; a painting on translucent fabric or paper illuminated from behind.</note>, and the Altar must be covered in black. Then three large triangular columns are required, whose names, written upon them as transparencies, are those of the three Theological virtues, so that Faith is in the West, Hope is in the South, and Charity is in the East.

Care must be taken that, to confer the Rose-Croix Degree, two other apartments are also required: one called the Room of Lost Steps <term>Chambre des Pas-Perdus</term> <note>In French architecture and Masonry, this refers to a waiting hall or lobby where candidates pace while waiting to be admitted.</note>, where there is only a writing table and seats for both the Candidate and those present; and the other called the Dark Chamber <term>Chambre obscure</term>, because it must indeed be dark enough that nothing can be seen. In this latter room, there are only chains, to intimidate the Candidate <term>Récipiendaire</term> while he travels <note>In Masonic ritual, "traveling" refers to the symbolic journey or "perambulation" the candidate performs during the initiation.</note>.

The Wardens <term>Surveillans</term> <note>Officers who supervise the two rows of members.</note> go, one to the North and the other to the South, and ask each Brother for the word, taking care to receive it in a low voice, at the ear, and not to make the sign. Having started from the West, they finish at the East by returning it to the Most Wise. They return to their places, and the Most Wise says: "Most Respectable first Knight, now that the word is found, what remains for us to do?"

> The first Knight. "Most Wise, to respect the decrees of the Most High, to pay homage to the Supreme Architect, and to humble ourselves unceasingly before everything that can reflect His image to us."

> The Most Wise. "Yes, Most Respectable Knights, that is the goal of our labors. My Brothers, let us bend the knee before Him who gave us being."

Upon saying these last words he rises, as does the entire Assembly. Everyone turns toward the East, makes the sign, bows, and places one knee on the ground. Immediately the Most Wise rises and everyone follows his example; then they clap their hands seven times, and say three times: *Huzza* <note>original: "Houzé." A phonetic French rendering of "Huzza" or "Hosanna," used as a ritual exclamation of acclamation or joy.</note>. This done, the Most Wise says: "Respectable Knights, the Sovereign Chapter is open."

### *Preparation of the Candidate.*

He is in the <term>Antechamber</term> <note>original: "chambre des Pas-perdus." Literally "Room of the Lost Steps," a traditional Masonic term for the waiting hall outside the lodge room.</note>, with the <term>Orator</term> and the <term>Master of Ceremonies</term>. The former gives him a short speech on the dignity of the <term>Degree</term> he is about to receive. Then he has him write his name and his profession, as well as the Degrees he holds in <term>Masonry</term>. For his age, they have him put thirty-three years <note>The age of 33 is highly symbolic in this degree, representing the traditional age of Jesus Christ at the time of the Resurrection.</note>.

The Master of Ceremonies goes to knock at the door as a <term>Knight of the East</term> <note>The Masonic degree immediately preceding that of Rose-Croix.</note>. The <term>Expert</term> <note>A ritual officer responsible for examining candidates.</note> who is inside answers him as a <term>Rose-Croix</term>, then informs the second Warden that someone is knocking at the door of the <term>Sovereign Chapter</term>. The second Warden tells the first, and the first tells the <term>Most Wise</term> <note>original: "Très-Sage." The title of the presiding officer of the Chapter.</note>. The latter orders to see who is knocking, and after the answer, they proceed to the reception in the usual manner; and when there is no opponent, they applaud with seven claps: then they say three times *Huzza* <note>original: "Houzé." A phonetic French rendering of "Huzza" or "Hosanna," used as a ritual exclamation.</note>. After which the Expert is ordered to let the Candidate enter. Immediately the Master of Ceremonies puts the sash of the Knight of the East on him and

Most Respectable Knight, deign to show the worthy Knight the means he must use to recover the **Word** <note>The "Word" (la parole) refers to the "Lost Word" of Freemasonry, symbolizing divine truth or the secret knowledge of the Master Mason that was lost through the death of Hiram Abiff.</note>. «

of Ceremonies takes the **Candidate** <term>original: "Récipiendaire"; the person receiving the degree</term> by the hand, leads him to the **Dark Chamber** <note>The "Chambre obscure" represents a state of symbolic mourning, ignorance, or the darkness of the tomb before the "rebirth" into higher knowledge.</note>, and has him walk around it seven times. Verse: Come to the foot of this Altar to receive the reward that is due to you.

> “By virtue of the power I have received from the **Metropolitan Lodge of Heredom** <note>Heredom, or Hérédon, is a mythical mountain in Scotland central to the legends of high-degree Masonry, particularly the Rose-Croix.</note>, and before this august assembly of **Knights**, my Brothers and my equals, I admit, receive, and constitute you, now and forever, **Knight, Prince of the Eagle and the Pelican, Perfect Free Mason of Heredom**, under the title of **Sovereign of the Rose-Cross**; that you may enjoy the titles and prerogatives of **Perfect Prince Masons** wherever there are Masons, with the power to hold Lodge in regularly assembled Lodges, to convene Lodge, and to make and perfect Masons up to the **Sixth Degree** <term>original: "sixieme Grade"</term>, or **Knight of the Sword**, also called **of the East**, without needing our authority, which we reserve solely for the **Rose-Cross Degree**.”

## CLOSING

**The Most Wise.** "Most Respectable First Knight, what time is it?"
**The First Knight.** "The last hour of the day."
**The Most Wise.** "If it is so, Most Respectable First and Second Knights, inform all our dear Brother Knights that it is the moment to close the sovereign Chapter, and to withdraw in peace."

The Wardens <term>original: *Surveillans*; the two officers who assist the Master in governing the Chapter</term> obey. Then the Most Wise strikes seven knocks in the Rose-Cross manner. The Wardens do the same. Immediately, the entire Assembly rises and makes the sign, looking toward the Most Wise, who also makes it. Then, still following the latter’s example, everyone claps their hands seven times and says three times *Huzza* <note>original: *Houzé*; a ritual acclamation used in several Masonic rites, often associated with joy or triumph</note>. ### *Third Point of the Rose-Cross.*

This Ceremony is always performed after having held a Chapter, as seen above. Therefore, when it is a day on which it must be observed, the **Most Wise** <note>The *Très-Sage* is the presiding officer of a Rose-Cross Chapter</note> does not close the Chapter. He only suspends it at the moment the hour is requested. All the decoration remains in the same state. Additionally, in the middle of the room, a table is placed, covered with a cloth, upon which there is a loaf of bread and a cup full of wine. Care is taken to proportion the size of the bread and the quantity of the wine so that each Brother

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new section or degree within the manuscript. The previous page concluded the Seventh Degree (Rose-Cross) with the symbolic burning of the sacred word. # THE NOACHITE,

->OR<-

## PRUSSIAN KNIGHT.

The very ancient Order of the Noachites, known under the name of Prussian Knight, translated from the German by Mr. de Bérage, Knight of Eloquence <term>an official orator in a Masonic lodge</term>, of the Lodge of Mr. de Saint-Gillair, Commander-Lieutenant, Inspector General of the Prussian Lodges in France, in the year of the Order 4658 <note>Masonic calendars often use their own dating systems; "4658" likely refers to a chronology starting from a biblical event like the Deluge or the creation of the world</note>.

---

### *Origin and Dignity.*

The Grand Master General of the Order, who is named Knight Grand Commander, is the most illustrious Brother Frederick William, King of Prussia <note>Referring to the Prussian monarchs who were celebrated patrons of Freemasonry in the 18th century</note>. His ancestors, for three hundred years, have been protectors of this Order whose Knights celebrate the memory of the destruction of the Tower of Babel. Formerly they were known by the name of **Noachites** <term>derived from Noah; the degree focuses on the "Noachide laws" and the era before the division of languages</term>; that is to say, descendants of Noah. The pagans [them...]

<sig>N 2</sig>

<summary>This title page introduces the Noachite or Prussian Knight degree, claiming historical protection by the Prussian monarchy and tracing its legendary origins to Noah and the Tower of Babel.</summary>
<keywords>Noachite, Prussian Knight, Frederick William, Tower of Babel, Noah, Mr. de Bérage, Masonic degrees, Knight of Eloquence</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Noachite" or "Prussian Knight" degree. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how pagans viewed the descendants of Noah.</meta>

( 148 )

...know under the name of Titans, who sought to scale the heavens to dethrone Jupiter. But the Prussians, who recognize no other God than the Grand Architect of the Universe <term>a non-denominational Masonic name for the Deity</term>, find their happiness in glorifying and celebrating every year, during the night of the full moon of March, the confusion of languages and the scattering of the workers of the Tower of Babel. This is considered one of the great wonders of the Creator, because it marks the era of that day of vengeance <note>In this degree, the "day of vengeance" relates to the divine punishment for the pride shown at Babel</note>. It is also for this reason that they gather in a secluded place on the night of the full moon each month to hold their Lodge, and they may only receive proselytes <term>newly admitted members or candidates</term> by the light of the moon.

The Grand Master General of the Order is titled Knight Commander-Lieutenant. The Officers are: the first Knight of Office, the second Knight of Office-Introducer, the third Knight of Eloquence <note>The official orator of the lodge</note>, the fourth Knight of the Guard, the fifth Knight of the Chancery, and the sixth Knight of Finance. The other Masters of the Lodge are called Prussian Knight-Masons. The Lodge must be composed of at least the first three Knights; however, this is only permitted in the event of a scarcity...

The Noachites <note>Followers of Noah, referring to the "Noachide Laws" or the lineage of Noah</note>, named today Prussian Knights, descend from Phaleg <note>The biblical Peleg; in Masonic tradition, he is often cited as the architect of the Tower of Babel</note>, the Grand Architect of the Tower of Babel; thus their Order draws its origin from further back than the Masons descending from Adonhiram <note>A central figure in Freemasonry, sometimes identified with Hiram Abiff, the builder of Solomon's Temple</note>; for the Tower of Babel was built several centuries before Solomon’s Temple, and in former times it was not required that the subjects who proposed themselves to be received be Masons descending from Adonhiram. 

But during the time of the Crusades, when all the Knights of the various Orders of Europe were initiated by the Christian Princes and confederated to conquer Palestine, the Masons descending from Adonhiram, named Adonhiramites <term>A branch of Freemasonry that focuses on the lineage and leadership of Adonhiram</term>, out of respect for the Order of the Noachites, which was held in great veneration at that time, sought admission. The Prussian Knights, out of gratitude, believing they could not better entrust their mysteries than to the descendants of Adonhiram, have since required that all Candidates <term>from the French "récipiendaires," those about to receive the degree</term> be received as Masters of that Order, and that no others may be admitted; as appears in the Statutes of the Order which are kept in the Archives of the King of Prussia, by

### *Arrangement of the Lodge and Reception.*

The Knight Commander is placed opposite the Moon, with the four Knights in front, to be better positioned to hear the orders. They have no fixed place, to demonstrate that a Knight who has renounced pride takes glory in practicing humility at all times. The Hall must be lit by at least one large window, positioned so that it may receive the faint light of the Moon. It is forbidden, according to the Statutes of the Order, to receive the rays of the Sun, or any artificial light. The Knight Commander-Lieutenant opens the Lodge with three knocks, struck very slowly, at equal intervals. The first Knight in office responds with a single blow which he strikes on the pommel of his sword. After which, the Commander-Lieutenant

### *Catechism of the Prussian Knight.*

Q. Who are you?
A. Tell me who you are, and I will tell you who I am.
Q. Do you know the children of Noah?
A. I know three of them. <note>In the context of this degree, these are Shem, Ham, and Japheth, the biblical survivors of the Great Flood.</note>
Q. Who are they?
A. I will say it through all the words of S. C. J. <note>original: "S. C. J." — These initials represent the French names of Noah's sons: Sem, Cham, and Japhet.</note>
Q. Tell me the word?
A. You begin, and I will answer you.
(*The three words S. C. J. are given.*)
Q. What do these words mean?
A. The initial letters are the sacred word.
Q. Give me the grip? <note>original: "l’attouchement" — In Masonic rituals, this refers to a specific way of shaking hands or touching to prove one's rank.</note>
A. Here it is, by pressing three times, etc.
Q. Give me the sign?
A. I will comply.
(*The sign is performed.*)
Q. Tell me the password?
A. Phaleg. <note>Phaleg (or Peleg) is a biblical figure mentioned in Genesis; in this tradition, he is identified as the architect of the Tower of Babel.</note>
Q. Do you know the Grand Architect of the Tower of Babel?
A. Phaleg is his name.
Q. Who taught you his history?
A. The Knight Commander-Lieutenant of the Prussian Master Knights?
Q. In what Lodge?

<summary>This page presents the opening of the ritual catechism for the Prussian Knight degree, establishing the candidate's identity through references to the sons of Noah and the biblical figure Phaleg.</summary>
<keywords>Prussian Knight, Noachite, Catechism, Noah, Phaleg, Tower of Babel, Masonic ritual, S.C.J.</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Catechism" or ritualized instructional dialogue for the Prussian Knight degree, also known as the Noachite. It explores the moral lessons derived from the story of the Tower of Babel—specifically the themes of human pride versus divine mercy.</meta>

**A.** In a Lodge where the Moon gave the light. <note>Noachite meetings were traditionally held at night, often on the night of a full moon, symbolizing a connection to the celestial cycles and a time of reflection or "lesser light."</note>
**Q.** Could one not have had any other light?
**A.** No.
**Q.** Was this building praiseworthy? <note>This refers to the Tower of Babel, mentioned on the previous page.</note>
**A.** No, its perfection was impossible.
**Q.** Why was it impossible?
**A.** It is because pride was its first foundation.
**Q.** Is it to imitate the children of Noah that you preserve the memory of it?
**A.** No; it is to keep their fault before our eyes.
**Q.** Where does the body of Phaleg rest?
**A.** In a tomb.
**Q.** Was he tested?
**A.** No: the agate stone says that God had pity on him, because he became humble. <note>In the lore of this degree, an agate stone was allegedly discovered in the 18th century among ancient ruins, bearing Hebrew inscriptions that told the story of Phaleg’s repentance for his role in building the Tower.</note>
**Q.** How were you received as a Prussian Knight?
**A.** By three genuflections, after having kissed the pommel of the sword of the Knight Commander-Lieutenant.
**Q.** Why did he have you perform genuflections?
**A.** To make me remember that, in all my life, I must practice humility.
**Q.** Why do the Knights wear a triangle?

<summary>This page of the ritual focuses on the moral failure of the Tower of Babel, identifying pride as the cause of its collapse. It emphasizes humility as the core virtue of the Prussian Knight, symbolized by the candidate's genuflections and the biblical figure Phaleg's repentance.</summary>
<keywords>Tower of Babel, pride, humility, Phaleg, agate stone, genuflection, Prussian Knight, Noachite ritual, Masonic catechism</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the catechism of the Prussian Knight (Noachite) degree, specifically focusing on the symbols and tools used by the order to commemorate the history of the Tower of Babel and the lessons of humility. It follows the question from the previous page regarding the significance of the triangle.</meta>

**A.** In memory of the Temple of Phaleg. <note>In the lore of this degree, Phaleg is identified as the architect of the Tower of Babel who later repented his pride and retreated to a life of humble contemplation.</note>
**Q.** Why the inverted arrow? <note>The downward-pointing arrow is a primary symbol of this degree, representing divine justice or the "thunderbolt" that struck the Tower of Babel to halt the work of the prideful.</note>
**A.** In memory of what happened to the Tower of Babel. The **Apron** and the **Gloves** are in memory of all the **Workmen** of the tower. The black sash <note>original: "cordon"</note> is a mark of sorrow. <note>The black sash symbolizes mourning for the confusion of languages and the loss of the original unity of mankind.</note>
**Q.** Do the **Workmen** work day and night?
**A.** Yes; by day, by the favor of the sun’s rays; and at night, by the favor of the moon. <note>This implies that the pursuit of wisdom and the labor of the Order are constant, guided by both the "greater light" of reason and the "lesser light" of reflection or revelation.</note>
