# HISTORY
## OF THE NOACHITES
### OR
## PRUSSIAN KNIGHTS.

**The** descendants of Noah, despite the rainbow which was the sign of reconciliation that the Lord had given to men, by which he assured them that he would no longer take vengeance upon them by a universal flood, resolved to build a tower high enough to shelter themselves from divine vengeance. For this purpose, they chose a plain named Sennaar <note>original: "Sennaar"; usually referred to in English as Shinar, located in Mesopotamia.</note>, in Asia. Ten years after they had laid the foundations of this edifice, the Lord—says the Scripture—cast His eyes upon the earth, perceived the pride of the children of men, and descended to the earth to confound their rash projects, and placed the confusion of languages among the workmen; this is why this tower is called Babel, which signifies confusion. Some time later, Nimrod,

<sig>O 3</sig>

<summary>This page begins the legendary history of the Noachites, recounting the biblical story of the Tower of Babel. It describes how Noah's descendants, fearing another flood despite the promise of the rainbow, attempted to build a tower in the plain of Shinar to escape divine wrath, leading to the confusion of languages.</summary>
<keywords>Noachites, Prussian Knights, Noah, rainbow, Tower of Babel, Sennaar, Shinar, Nimrod, confusion of languages</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 161, following the mention of Nimrod. It transitions from the biblical event of the confusion of languages to the specific legendary origins of the Noachite ritual practices and the penance of Phaleg.</meta>

who was the first to establish distinctions among men, and who even avenged the rights and the worship due to the Divinity, founded a city there, which, for this reason, was called Babylon; that is to say, an enclosure of confusion <note>original: "enceinte de confusion"; the text links the name Babylon to the Hebrew root 'balal', meaning to mix or confound.</note>. It was on the night of the full Moon of March that the Lord worked this miracle. It is in memory of this that the Noachite Knights hold their grand assembly every year during the full Moon of March. Their instructional assemblies are held every month, on the day of the full moon and by the light of the Moon, as they may have no other light in the Lodge than the Moon.

The workmen, no longer understanding one another, were obliged to separate. Each went their own way. *Phaleg* <note>In the Bible, Peleg (Phaleg) is a descendant of Noah; in Masonic legend, he is often identified as the architect of the Tower of Babel.</note>, who had given the idea for this building and who was its director, was the most guilty. He condemned himself to a rigorous penance; he retired to the North of *Germany*, where he arrived after many hardships and fatigues endured in desert lands, where he found nothing for food but roots and wild fruits.

In this region that is called *Prussia*, he built several huts to shelter himself from the harshness of the weather and the air, and a Temple in the shape of a triangle, where he shut himself away to implore

<summary>This page explains the origins of Noachite lunar traditions, linking their assemblies to the full moon of March when the confusion of languages occurred. It also recounts the self-imposed exile of Phaleg, the architect of Babel, to Prussia, where he lived in penance and built a triangular temple.</summary>
<keywords>Babylon, Noachite Knights, March full moon, Phaleg, Germany, Prussia, triangular temple, penance, Tower of Babel</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page concludes the ritual legend of the Noachite or Prussian Knight degree. It picks up from the previous page where Phaleg, the legendary architect of the Tower of Babel, has retreated to Prussia to live in penance and prayer.</meta>

the mercy of God and the remission of his sin.

Among some ruins, while digging in the salt mines of Prussia at a depth of fifteen cubits <term>cubit: an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</term>, in the year 553, a triangular-shaped structure was found. Inside was a piece of white marble, on the base of which the entire history was written in Hebrew. Beside this column, a sandstone slab was found, where dust was seen, along with an agate stone upon which was the following epitaph:

> *Here rest the ashes of our **G. A.** <note>G. A. stands for "Grand Architect" (Grand Architecte).</note> of the tower of Babel. The Lord had mercy on him, because he became humble.*

All these monuments are in the possession of the King of Prussia. The epitaph does not state that Phaleg was the Architect of the tower of Babel; however, the history found on the base of this column informs us that Phaleg was the son of Eber <note>original: "Hébert"; the biblical Eber, the ancestor of the Hebrews.</note>, whose father was the son of Arphaxad, who was the son of Shem <note>original: "Sem".</note>, the eldest son of Noah. Password: Phaleg. Sacred word: S. C. J., which signify Shem, Ham, and Japheth <note>The three sons of Noah: Shem (Sem), Ham (Cham), and Japheth (Japhet).</note>.

->*End of the second Volume.*<-

# J E W E L <note>The French term "Bijou" refers to the ritual insignia or medal worn by members of this Masonic degree.</note>

Among some ruins, while digging in the salt mines of Prussia at a depth of fifteen cubits <term>cubit: an ancient unit of length based on the length of the forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</term>, in the year 553, a triangular-shaped structure was found. Inside was found a piece of white marble, on the base of which the entire history was written in Hebrew. Beside this column, a sandstone slab was found, where dust was seen, along with an agate stone upon which was the following epitaph:

> *Here rest the ashes of our **G. A.** <note>G. A. stands for "Grand Architect" (Grand Architecte).</note> of the Tower of Babel. The Lord had mercy on him, because he became humble.*

All these monuments are in the possession of the King of Prussia. The epitaph does not state that Phaleg was the Architect of the Tower of Babel; however, the history found on the base of this column informs us that Phaleg was the son of Eber <note>original: "Hébert"; the biblical Eber, the ancestor of the Hebrews.</note>, whose father was the son of Arphaxad, who was the son of Shem <note>original: "Sem".</note>, the eldest son of Noah. Password: Phaleg. Sacred word: S. C. J., which signify Shem, Ham, and Japheth <note>The three sons of Noah: Shem (Sem), Ham (Cham), and Japheth (Japhet).</note>.

->*End of the second Volume.*<-

## THE SECRET OF THE FREEMASONS.

->Tune: *I love a witty word.*<- <note>original: "J'aime le mot pour rire." This indicates the song was set to a popular contemporary melody, a common practice in Masonic festive songbooks.</note>

I have not, until this time,
Allowed my timid voice
To sing of our mysteries;
But, if I believe what I have seen,
Building a temple to Virtue,
That is the secret,
That is the secret,
That is the secret of the Brothers.

The square in hand, each worker,
Adorned with a simple apron,
Works on the edifice;
And so that in this moment
Virtue may be more fittingly housed,
They build there,
They build there,
Dungeons for vice. <note>The phrase "to build temples to virtue and dungeons for vice" is a classic Masonic maxim, emphasizing the internal struggle to improve one's character.</note>

If this Temple of Solomon
Is not the heart of a true Mason,
I know very little of it;
Each day, from conquered vice,
Offering homage to Virtue;

->That is the secret,<-
->That is the secret of a good Brother.<- <note>Masonic charity is traditionally intended to be practiced quietly and without seeking public recognition.</note>

->Ranks, titles, dignities, greatness,<-
->Elsewhere, let them take the place of happiness,<-
->Here, you are forgotten;<-
->Ranged under the same banners,<-
->Princes and subjects are all equal;<- <note>The concept of "meeting on the level" meant that within the Lodge, social hierarchy was theoretically set aside in favor of fraternal equality.</note>
->That is the secret,<-
->That is the secret,<-
->Of Masonry.<-

## H Y M N .

->Tune: *Have you seen my beloved?* <note>original: "L'avez-vous vu mon bien-aimé ?" This was a popular 18th-century melody, likely from a contemporary comic opera or vaudeville, used here as a contrafactum (new lyrics set to a known tune).</note><-

To find happiness,
One constantly torments oneself;
This treasure, so widely desired everywhere,
Is only found where the soul is steadfast.
Among good Brothers, among Friends,
Everything seems to take on a new value;
->Each moment,<-
->Of feeling,<-
Bears a lively imprint,
->And without effort,<-
->From a sweet rapture,<-
The soul feels the touch.

Of honor, of morals, of virtues,
These are our titles, nothing more:
->Every citizen,<-
->Doing good,<-
A good Friend, a good Husband, a good Father,
Is a true Mason <term>Mason: A member of the Freemasons, a fraternal organization focused on moral improvement and charity.</term>, and a good Brother.

Within our tranquil hearths <note>original: "foyers." While it literally means "homes" or "fireplaces," in this context it refers to the Lodge itself as a safe, domestic-like space for the Masonic family.</note>,
Wisdom presides:
Our Wardens <term>Wardens: Referred to in French as *Surveillans*, these are two of the principal officers of a Lodge who assist the Master in governing the members.</term>, our Officers,
Have Friendship for their guide.

<vocab>Hymn, happiness, Brothers, Mason, Father, Wardens, Officers, Friendship</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a Masonic hymn that defines the "true Mason" not by secret titles, but by his virtues as a citizen, friend, husband, and father. It emphasizes the Lodge as a place of wisdom and friendship where members find the happiness that eludes them in the outside world.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Masonic Hymn, Enlightenment Virtues, Citizenship, Domesticity, Wardens, Masonic Officers, Moral Conduct, Happiness</keywords>

Esteem has guided our choice.
How could we not cherish our laws? <note>original: "loix." This is an archaic spelling of the French "lois."</note>
->In her labors<-
->Always consistent,<-
The industrious and steady Bee, <note>The Bee and the Hornet were popular 18th-century symbols; the bee represents the hardworking, virtuous Mason, while the hornet represents the idle or malicious outsider who "murmurs" or complains without contributing.</note>
->Sees from her corner,<-
->Without fear, in the distance,<-
The murmuring Hornet.

Within a pleasant interior,
We find peace and sweetness:
->Living in harmony,<-
->One is very strong,<-
It is easy, when we love one another,
To be self-sufficient.

Let us be, as faithful Masons <term>Masons: Members of the Masonic fraternity, a group focused on mutual aid and moral progress.</term>,
United for life:
Our true friends will remain with us,
Despite dark envy:
Let us love one another, and fear nothing,
This is the true, the greatest good.
->Celestial gift!<-
->Tender Union,<-
We raise this Temple <term>Temple: In Masonic thought, the "Temple" is rarely a physical building; it is a symbolic structure of a perfect society or the moral character of the individual member.</term> to you;
->To jealous hearts,<-
->Of a good so sweet,<-
You shall serve as an example.

# HYMN TO FRIENDSHIP.

->Tune: *From the Romance of Gabrielle.*<- <note>original: "De la Romance de Gabrielle." This refers to a popular 18th-century song, "Charmante Gabrielle," often attributed to King Henri IV.</note>

Sublime harmony of souls,
Source of true happiness,
Kindle with your flames
Our feeling hearts: <note>original: "sensible." In the 18th century, "sensible" meant having a great capacity for emotion and empathy, a key virtue of the "Man of Feeling."</note>
Friendship, sweet and tender,
->Come forever<-
->To pour upon us here<-
->All your benefits.<-

It is through you that we taste
Pure delight: <note>original: "volupté." In this context, it refers to a refined, spiritual pleasure rather than a carnal one.</note>
Time unceasingly adds <note>original: "tems." An archaic spelling of the French "temps."</note>
A luster to your beauty,
Everything becomes enjoyment
->In your sweet bonds,<- <term>bonds: The original "nœuds" (knots) refers to the symbolic ties that bind Masons together in a "chain of union."</term>
And your constancy alone
->Makes us happy.<-

Of the flame of love
You have only the attractions,
But also in our soul,
You prevent regrets:
When love overwhelms us
->With its rigors,<-

<summary>This page introduces a new Masonic hymn dedicated to Friendship, celebrating it as a source of sublime harmony and refined pleasure. The lyrics contrast the steady, lasting joy of friendship with the potentially overwhelming "rigors" of romantic love.</summary>
<keywords>Friendship, harmony, emotion, Romance of Gabrielle, virtue, constancy</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Hymn to Friendship" from page 172. The previous page ended by contrasting the "rigors" of romantic love with the stability of friendship; this page concludes the hymn by praising friendship's universal reach and its ability to restore a "Golden Age" of virtue.</meta>

<page-num>( 173 )</page-num>

Your ineffable sweetness
Dries our tears.

You dissipate fears,
You banish remorse,
You brave the assaults
And the rigors of fate:
From one pole to the other <note>original: "pôle." This imagery emphasizes the universal nature of the Masonic brotherhood, which claims to span the entire globe.</note>
Your divine name,
Consoles of all his ills
The true Mason. <term>Mason: original: "Maçon." Referring to the initiate who practices the virtues of the craft.</term>

Of cruel envy
You confound the darkness,
Upon the winter of life
You know how to sow flowers:
You serve youth
As a steady guide,
Preserve for our old age
A pure pleasure.

Become here the pledge
Of a tender union,
Disperse every cloud
From this pure horizon: <note>original: "horison." An archaic spelling of the French "horizon."</note>
From the ethereal vault,
Come, forever
To bring back to us
The happy days of Astraea. <note>original: "Astrée." Astraea was the Greek goddess of Justice and Innocence. According to myth, she was the last of the immortals to leave Earth as the Golden Age ended. Her "return" is a common Masonic metaphor for the restoration of virtue and peace through the work of the Lodge.</note>

<sig>P 3</sig>

<vocab>Mason, Astraea, horizon, union, sweetness</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the "Hymn to Friendship," portraying friendship as a divine force that protects Masons from fate and envy. It ends with a classical invocation of Astraea, symbolizing a desire to return to a legendary Golden Age of justice and peace.</summary>
<keywords>Friendship, universalism, Astraea, Golden Age, virtue, Masonic brotherhood</keywords>

### ANOTHER.

To the tune of: *Frivolous and Fickle Love*, or
->*The Fanfare of Saint-Cloud.*<-

You of Masonry,
O wise founders,
Who have regulated the honors
Of our artillery, <note>In 18th-century Masonic banquets, "artillery" (artillerie) was a playful term for the ritualized drinking of toasts. Toasts were called "cannon shots," and the wine was "powder."</note>
Be favorable to my songs;
A temple is built
Under the laws and the auspices
Of celestial Friendship.

To Thalia, to Melpomene, <note>Thalia was the Greek Muse of comedy and pastoral poetry; Melpomene was the Muse of tragedy.</note>
Let others pay their court:
The Muse who binds us
Is that of tender love;
To this love, so vast,
Everything is by us sacrificed;
Love, beneficent and chaste,
Sweet and celestial Friendship.

In vain the ignorant vulgar <note>original: "l'ignorant vulgaire." A common term in Masonic texts for those outside the brotherhood (the "profane") who do not understand its mysteries.</note>
Seek to sound our secret;
The character of the Mason
Is to be always discreet;
To hide the good he can do,

<vocab>Masonry, artillery, Friendship, Thalia, Melpomene, Mason, discreet</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a new Masonic song that honors the founders of the Craft and clarifies that Masonic "artillery" (ritual toasts) is dedicated to Friendship. It contrasts the Masonic focus on "chaste love" with the classical Muses and emphasizes the importance of discretion against the curiosity of the uninitiated.</summary>
<keywords>Masonic songs, ritual toasts, artillery, Thalia, Melpomene, secrecy, Friendship</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the song from page 174. The final stanza on the previous page ended with the Mason's duty "to hide the good he can do," and this page completes that thought before continuing with the duties of the Mason and the Master of the Lodge.</meta>

<page-num>( 175 )</page-num>

Never to do so by halves,
To tenderly love one’s Brother,
With a celestial Friendship.

In the Masonic arena <note>original: "lice." This term refers to the "lists" or field of combat in a tournament, here metaphorically describing the space where Masons practice their craft.</note>
He follows the beaten path;
Builds dungeons for vice,
And temples to virtue: <note>A central Masonic allegory: "to build temples to virtue and dig dungeons for vice."</note>
Inclined toward benevolence,
And sensitive to piety,
He feels within him the presence
Of celestial Friendship.

To preside over amiable Brothers,
To instruct them, to form them,
To reveal our mysteries to them,
Above all, that of loving one another?
Wise and worshipful Master, <note>original: "respectable Maître," the formal title for the presiding officer of a French Lodge.</note>
This care is entrusted to you:
What fruits you will see born
From their celestial Friendship.

To the plumb-rule, <note>original: "perpendiculaire," one of the "immovable jewels" of the Lodge, symbolizing uprightness.</note>
You will unite the level:
Of the compasses and the square,
You will show them the meaning:
These jewels are the compass <note>original: "boussole," meaning a magnetic navigation compass.</note>
Of every initiated Brother;
They always point toward the pole
Of celestial Friendship.

<summary>This page concludes the song praising Masonic Friendship, detailing the moral duties of a Mason—to fight vice and cultivate virtue—and the specific role of the Worshipful Master in using the symbolic tools (plumb, level, square, and compass) to guide the lodge.</summary>
<keywords>Masonic tools, plumb-rule, level, square and compass, Worshipful Master, virtue and vice, benevolence, celestial Friendship</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Masonic song that began on page 174. While the previous page focused on the tools and duties of the Worshipful Master, this final section provides a closing exhortation on the execution of Masonic "designs" and a personal oath of brotherhood.</meta>

<page-num>( 176 )</page-num>

With prudence and wisdom;
You will trace your designs; <note>In a Masonic context, "designs" refers to the moral and architectural plans drawn by the Master on the Tracing Board for the workers to follow.</note>
With strength, with nobility,
You will execute them.
For the beauty of the work
If you are envied,
You will ward off the storm
Through celestial Friendship.

My Brothers, I swear it to you;
With the most vibrant passion,
This sweet and pure Friendship,
Is the source of our happiness.
As long as the sphere <note>original: "la sphere." This refers to the globe or the universe, a common symbol in the Lodge representing the reach of Masonic principles.</note> shall last,
I shall always be bound
By the most sincere love
To celestial Friendship.
